H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d+1},\mathbb{C})
as follows

\Pi_{N}\rho=\sum_{\underset{\langle l,j\rangle\leqslant N}{(l,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d+1}}}\rho_{l,j}\mathbf{e}_{l,j}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\Pi^{\perp}_{N}=\textnormal{Id}-\Pi_{N}.

(4.1)

We shall also make use of the following mixed weighted Sobolev spaces with respect to the parameter
\gamma
.
Let
\mathcal{O}
be an open bounded set of
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
and define

\displaystyle W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{s})

\displaystyle=\Big\{\rho:\mathcal{O}\rightarrow H^{s}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{s.t.}\hskip 10.00002pt\|\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}<\infty\Big\},

\displaystyle W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle=\Big\{\rho:\mathcal{O}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{s.t.}\hskip 10.00002pt\|\rho\|_{q}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}<\infty\Big\},

where for
\mu\in\mathcal{O}\mapsto\rho(\mu)\in H^{s}
or
\mu\in\mathcal{O}\mapsto\rho(\mu)\in\mathbb{C}
the norm is defined by

\displaystyle\|\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\underset{|\alpha|\leqslant q}{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}}}\gamma^{|\alpha|}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{O}}}\|\partial_{\mu}^{\alpha}\rho(\mu,\cdot)\|_{H^{s-|\alpha|}},

\displaystyle\|\rho\|_{q}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\underset{|\alpha|\leqslant q}{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}}}\gamma^{|\alpha|}\sup_{\mu\in{\mathcal{O}}}|\partial_{\mu}^{\alpha}\rho(\mu)|.

(4.2)

Remark 4.1
.

•

From Sobolev embeddings we obtain,

W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{C})\hookrightarrow C^{q-1}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{C}).

•

The spaces
\left(W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{s}),\|\cdot\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\right)
and
\left(W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{C}),\|\cdot\|_{q}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\right)
are complete.

•

For needs related to the use of the kernels of integral operators, we will have to duplicate the variable
\theta
. Thus we may define the weighted Sobolev space
W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s})
similarly as above and denote the corresponding norm by
\|\cdot\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

The next lemma gathers some useful classical results related to various operations in weighted Sobolev spaces. The proofs are standard and can be found for instance in
[
13
,
14
,
16
]
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
(\gamma,q,d,s_{0},s)
satisfy (
1.11
), then the following assertions hold true.

(i)

Space translation invariance: Let
\rho\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{s}),
then for all
\eta\in\mathbb{T},
the function
(\varphi,\theta)\mapsto\rho(\varphi,\eta+\theta)
belongs to
W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{s})
, and satisfies

\|\rho(\cdot,\eta+\cdot)\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}=\|\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(ii)

Projectors properties: Let
\rho\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{s}),
then for all
N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and for all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*},

\|\Pi_{N}\rho\|_{q,s+t}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\leqslant N^{t}\|\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\|\Pi_{N}^{\perp}\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\leqslant N^{-t}\|\rho\|_{q,s+t}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}},

where the projectors are defined in (
4.1
).

(iii)

Interpolation inequality:
Let
q<s_{1}\leqslant s_{3}\leqslant s_{2}
and
\overline{\theta}\in[0,1],
with
s_{3}=\overline{\theta}s_{1}+(1-\overline{\theta})s_{2}.

If
\rho\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{s_{2}})
, then
\rho\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{s_{3}})
and

\|\rho\|_{q,s_{3}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\left(\|\rho\|_{q,s_{1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\right)^{\overline{\theta}}\left(\|\rho\|_{q,s_{2}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\right)^{1-\overline{\theta}}.

(iv)

Law products:

(a)

Let
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{s}).
Then
\rho_{1}\rho_{2}\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{s})
and

\|\rho_{1}\rho_{2}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\rho_{1}\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\rho_{1}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(b)

Let
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{C}).
Then
\rho_{1}\rho_{2}\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{C})
and

\|\rho_{1}\rho_{2}\|_{q}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\rho_{1}\|_{q}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(c)

Let
(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{C})\times W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{s}).
Then
\rho_{1}\rho_{2}\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{s})
and

\|\rho_{1}\rho_{2}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\rho_{1}\|_{q}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(v)

Composition law 1: Let
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
and
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{s})
such that

\|\rho_{1}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}},\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\leqslant C_{0}

for an arbitrary constant
C_{0}>0
and define the pointwise composition

\forall(\mu,\varphi,\theta)\in\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{T}^{d+1},\hskip 10.00002ptf(\rho)(\mu,\varphi,\theta):=f(\mu,\rho(\mu,\varphi,\theta)).

Then
f(\rho_{1})-f(\rho_{2})\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{s})
with

\|f(\rho_{1})-f(\rho_{2})\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\leqslant C(s,d,q,f,C_{0})\|\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(vi)

Composition law 2: Let
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
with bounded derivatives. Let
\rho\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{C}).
Then

\|f(\rho)-f(0)\|_{q}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\leqslant C(q,d,f)\|\rho\|_{q}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\left(1+\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{O})}^{q-1}\right).

This estimate is also true for
\gamma=1
, corresponding to the classical Sobolev space
W^{q,\infty}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{C}).

The next result will be useful later in the study of the linearized operator.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
(\gamma,q,d,s_{0},s)
satifying (
1.11
) and
f\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{s}).

We consider the function
g:\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{T}_{\varphi}^{d}\times\mathbb{T}_{\theta}\times\mathbb{T}_{\eta}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
defined by

g(\mu,\varphi,\theta,\eta)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{f(\mu,\varphi,\eta)-f(\mu,\varphi,\theta)}{\sin\left(\tfrac{\eta-\theta}{2}\right)}&\textnormal{if }\theta\neq\eta\\
2\partial_{\theta}f(\mu,\varphi,\theta)&\textnormal{if }\theta=\eta.\end{array}\right.

Then

(i)

\forall k\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 10.00002pt\displaystyle\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{T}}\|(\partial_{\theta}^{k}g)(\ast,\cdot,\centerdot,\eta+\centerdot)\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\partial_{\theta}f\|_{q,s+k}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|f\|_{q,s+k+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(ii)

\|g\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|f\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

Proof.

(i)
This result has been proved in
[
47
, Lem. 4.2]
.

(ii)
It suffices to prove the case
q=0.
Recall the following classical norm estimate

\|g\|_{H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s}}\lesssim\|g\|_{H_{\varphi,\theta}^{s}L_{\eta}^{2}}+\|g\|_{L_{\theta}^{2}H_{\varphi,\eta}^{s}}.

(4.3)

By the translation invariance property

\displaystyle\|g\|_{L_{\theta}^{2}H_{\varphi,\eta}^{s}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\|g(\cdot,\theta+\centerdot,\centerdot)\|_{H_{\varphi,\eta}^{s}}^{2}d\theta

\displaystyle\lesssim\;\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{T}}\|g(\ast,\cdot,\theta+\centerdot,\centerdot)\|_{H_{\varphi,\eta}^{s}}.

Using the first point and the symmetry
g
in
(\eta,\theta)
we obtain

\|g\|_{L_{\theta}^{\infty}H_{\varphi,\eta}^{s}}\lesssim\|f\|_{s+1}.

Introducing the Bessel potential
J^{s}
defined in Fourier by

\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},\hskip 10.00002pt(J^{s}u)_{j}=\max(1,|j|)^{s}u_{j},

(4.4)

a use of Fubini’s Theorem implies

\displaystyle\|g\|_{H_{\varphi,\theta}^{s}L_{\eta}^{2}}=\|J_{\varphi,\theta}^{s}\,g\|_{L_{\varphi}^{2}L_{\theta}^{2}L_{\eta}^{2}}=\|J_{\varphi,\theta}^{s}\,g\|_{L_{\eta}^{2}L_{\varphi}^{2}L_{\theta}^{2}}=\|g\|_{L_{\eta}^{2}H_{\varphi,\theta}^{s}}.

Since
g
is symmetric in the variables
\theta
and
\eta,
we get

\|g\|_{L_{\eta}^{2}H_{\varphi,\theta}^{s}}=\|g\|_{L_{\theta}^{2}H_{\varphi,\eta}^{s}}.

Combining the foregoing estimates leads to

\|g\|_{H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s}}\lesssim\|f\|_{s+1}.

This ends the proof of Lemma
4.2
.
∎

We now turn to the presentation of quasi-periodic symplectic change of variables needed for the reduction of the transport part of the linearized operator in the construction of the approximate inverse in the normal directions.
Let
\beta:\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{T}^{d+1}\to\mathbb{T}
be a smooth function such that
\displaystyle\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{O}}\|\beta(\mu,\cdot,\centerdot)\|_{\textnormal{Lip}}<1

then the map

(\varphi,\theta)\in\mathbb{T}^{d+1}\mapsto(\varphi,\theta+\beta(\mu,\varphi,\theta))\in\mathbb{T}^{d+1}

is a diffeomorphism with inverse having the form

(\varphi,\theta)\in\mathbb{T}^{d+1}\mapsto(\varphi,\theta+\widehat{\beta}(\mu,\varphi,\theta))\in\mathbb{T}^{d+1}.

Moreover, one has the relation

y=\theta+\beta(\mu,\varphi,\theta)\Longleftrightarrow\theta=y+\widehat{\beta}(\mu,\varphi,y).

(4.5)

Define the operators

\mathscr{B}=(1+\partial_{\theta}\beta)\mathcal{B},

(4.6)

with

\mathcal{B}\rho(\mu,\varphi,\theta)=\rho\big(\mu,\varphi,\theta+\beta(\mu,\varphi,\theta)\big).

(4.7)

By straightforward computations we obtain

\mathscr{B}^{-1}\rho(\mu,\varphi,y)=\Big(1+\partial_{y}\widehat{\beta}(\mu,\varphi,y)\Big)\rho\big(\mu,\varphi,y+\widehat{\beta}(\mu,\varphi,y)\big)

(4.8)

and

\mathcal{B}^{-1}\rho(\mu,\varphi,y)=\rho\big(\mu,\varphi,y+\widehat{\beta}(\mu,\varphi,y)\big).

The following lemma gives some elementary algebraic properties for
\mathcal{B}^{\pm 1}
and
\mathscr{B}^{\pm 1}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

The following assertions hold true.

(i)

The action of
\mathscr{B}^{-1}
on the derivative is given by

\mathscr{B}^{-1}\partial_{\theta}=\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{B}^{-1}.

(ii)

Denote by
\mathscr{B}^{\star}
the
L^{2}_{\theta}(\mathbb{T})
-adjoint of
\mathscr{B}
, then

\mathscr{B}^{\star}=\mathcal{B}^{-1}\hskip 10.00002pt\hbox{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{B}^{\star}=\mathscr{B}^{-1}.

Now we shall state the following result proved in
[
47
, Lem. 6.2]
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
(q,d\gamma,s_{0})
as in (
1.11
).
Let
\beta\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}\big(\mathcal{O},H^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d+1})\big)
such that

\|\beta\|_{q,2s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\leqslant\varepsilon_{0},

(4.9)

with
\varepsilon_{0}
small enough. Then the following assertions hold true.

(i)

The linear operators
\mathcal{B},\mathscr{B}:W^{q,\infty,\gamma}\big(\mathcal{O},H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d+1})\big)\to W^{q,\infty,\gamma}\big(\mathcal{O},H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d+1})\big)
are continuous and invertible, with

\forall s\geqslant s_{0},\hskip 10.00002pt\|\mathcal{B}^{\pm 1}\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\leqslant\|\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\left(1+C\|\beta\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\right)+C\|\beta\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

(4.10)

and

\forall s\geqslant s_{0},\hskip 10.00002pt\|\mathscr{B}^{\pm 1}\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\leqslant\|\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\left(1+C\|\beta\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\right)+C\|\beta\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(4.11)

(ii)

The functions
\beta
and
\widehat{\beta}
are linked through

\forall s\geqslant s_{0},\hskip 10.00002pt\|\widehat{\beta}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\leqslant C\|\beta\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(4.12)

(iii)

Let
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d+1}))
satisfying (
4.9
). If we denote

\Delta_{12}\beta=\beta_{1}-\beta_{2}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\Delta_{12}\widehat{\beta}=\widehat{\beta}_{1}-\widehat{\beta}_{2},

then they are linked through

\forall s\geqslant s_{0},\hskip 10.00002pt\|\Delta_{12}\widehat{\beta}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\leqslant C\left(\|\Delta_{12}\beta\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\Delta_{12}\beta\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}\|\beta_{j}\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\right).

(4.13)

4.2
Operators

In this section we shall collect some basic definitions and properties related to suitable operators class.
Consider a smooth family of bounded operators on Sobolev spaces
H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d+1})
,

T:\mu=(b,\omega)\in\mathcal{O}\mapsto T(\mu)\in\mathcal{L}(H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d+1},\mathbb{C})).

The linear operator
T(\mu)
can be represented by the infinite dimensional matrix
\left(T_{l_{0},j_{0}}^{l,j}(\mu)\right)_{\underset{(j,j_{0})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}{(l,l_{0})\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{2}}}
with

T(\mu)\mathbf{e}_{l_{0},j_{0}}=\sum_{(l,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d+1}}T_{l_{0},j_{0}}^{l,j}(\mu)\mathbf{e}_{l,j}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{where}\hskip 10.00002ptT_{l_{0},j_{0}}^{l,j}(\mu)=\big\langle T(\mu)\mathbf{e}_{l_{0},j_{0}},\mathbf{e}_{l,j}\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d+1})}.

Along this paper the operators and the test functions may depend on the same parameter
\mu
and thus
the action of the operator
T(\mu)
on a scalar function
\rho\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{d+1},\mathbb{C}))
is by convention
defined through

(T\rho)(\mu,\varphi,\theta):=T(\mu)\rho(\mu,\varphi,\theta).

4.2.1
Toeplitz in time operators

In this paper we always consider Toeplitz in time operators, whose definition is the following.

Definition 4.1
.

An operator
T(\mu)
is said Toeplitz in time (actually in the variable
\varphi
) if its Fourier coefficients satisfy,

T_{l_{0},j_{0}}^{l,j}(\mu)=T_{0,j_{0}}^{l-l_{0},j}(\mu).

Or equivalently,

T_{l_{0},j_{0}}^{l,j}(\mu)=T_{j_{0}}^{j}(\mu,l-l_{0})\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with}\hskip 10.00002ptT_{j_{0}}^{j}(\mu,l):=T_{0,j_{0}}^{l,j}(\mu).

Notice that the action of a Toeplitz operator
T(\mu)
on a function
\rho=\displaystyle\sum_{(l_{0},j_{0})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d+1}}\rho_{l_{0},j_{0}}\mathbf{e}_{l_{0},j_{0}}
is given by

T(\mu)\rho=\sum_{(l,l_{0})\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{2}\\
\atop(j,j_{0})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}T_{j_{0}}^{j}(\mu,l-l_{0})\rho_{l_{0},j_{0}}\mathbf{e}_{l,j}.

(4.14)

For Toeplitz operators, we can define the off-diagonal norm as

\displaystyle\|T\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}=\sum_{\underset{|\alpha|\leqslant q}{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1}}}\gamma^{|\alpha|}\sup_{(b,\omega)\in{\mathcal{O}}}\|\partial_{\mu}^{\alpha}(T)(\mu)\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},s-|\alpha|},

(4.15)

where

\|T\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},s}^{2}=\sum_{(l,m)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d+1}}\langle l,m\rangle^{2s}\sup_{j-k=m}|T_{j}^{k}(l)|^{2}.

The cut-off projectors
(P_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
are defined as follows

\left(P_{N}T(\mu)\right)\mathbf{e}_{l_{0},j_{0}}=\sum_{\underset{|l-l_{0}|,|j-j_{0}|\leqslant N}{(l,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d+1}}}T_{l_{0},j_{0}}^{l,j}(\mu)\mathbf{e}_{l,j}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{and}\hskip 10.00002ptP_{N}^{\perp}T=T-P_{N}T.

(4.16)

The next lemma lists some classical results. The proofs are very similar to those in
[
16
]
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
(\gamma,q,d,s_{0},s)
satisfying (
1.11
). Let
T,

T_{1}
and
T_{2}
be Toeplitz in time operators.

(i)

Projectors properties : Let
N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}.
Let
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}.
Then

\|P_{N}T\rho\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s+t}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\leqslant N^{t}\|T\rho\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\|P_{N}^{\perp}T\rho\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\leqslant N^{-t}\|T\rho\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s+t}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(ii)

Interpolation inequality : Let
q<s_{1}\leqslant s_{3}\leqslant s_{2},\,\overline{\theta}\in[0,1]
with
s_{3}=\overline{\theta}s_{1}+(1-\overline{\theta})s_{2}.
Then

\|T\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s_{3}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\left(\|T\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s_{1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\right)^{\overline{\theta}}\left(\|T\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s_{2}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\right)^{1-\overline{\theta}}.

(iii)

Composition law :

\|T_{1}T_{2}\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|T_{1}\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|T_{2}\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|T_{1}\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|T_{2}\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(iv)

Link between operators and off-diagonal norms :

\|T\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|T\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|T\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

In particular

\|T\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|T\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

4.2.2
Reversible and reversibility preserving operators

We recall the following definitions of real reversible and reversibility preserving operators, see for instance
[
4
, Def. 2.2]
.

Definition 4.2
.

We define the following involution

(\mathscr{S}_{2}\rho)(\varphi,\theta)=\rho(-\varphi,-\theta).

(4.17)

We say that an operator
T(\mu)
is

•

real if for all
\rho\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d+1},\mathbb{C}),
we have

\overline{\rho}=\rho\hskip 10.00002pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\overline{T\rho}=T\rho.

•

reversible if

T(\mu)\circ\mathscr{S}_{2}=-\mathscr{S}_{2}\circ T(\mu).

•

reversibility preserving if

T(\mu)\circ\mathscr{S}_{2}=\mathscr{S}_{2}\circ T(\mu).

The following lemma gives characterizations of the reversibility notion in terms of Fourier coefficients. A similar result is stated in
[
4
, Lem. 2.6]
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
T
be an operator. Then
T
is

•

real if and only if

\forall(l,l_{0},j,j_{0})\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{2}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2},\hskip 10.00002ptT_{-l_{0},-j_{0}}^{-l,-j}=\overline{T_{l_{0},j_{0}}^{l,j}}.

•

reversible if and only if

\forall(l,l_{0},j,j_{0})\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{2}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2},\hskip 10.00002ptT_{-l_{0},-j_{0}}^{-l,-j}=-T_{l_{0},j_{0}}^{l,j}.

•

reversibility-preserving if and only if

\forall(l,l_{0},j,j_{0})\in(\mathbb{Z}^{d})^{2}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2},\hskip 10.00002ptT_{-l_{0},-j_{0}}^{-l,-j}=T_{l_{0},j_{0}}^{l,j}.

Throughout this paper we shall constantly make use of two kinds of operators : multiplication and integral operators.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
T
be an operator as in Section
\ref{section-ope}.

We say that

•

T
is a multiplication operator if there exists a function
M:(\mu,\varphi,\theta)\mapsto M(\mu,\varphi,\theta)
such that

(T\rho)(\mu,\varphi,\theta)=M(\mu,\varphi,\theta)\rho(\mu,\varphi,\theta).

•

T
is an integral operator if there exists a function (called the kernel)
K:(\mu,\varphi,\theta,\eta)\mapsto K(\mu,\varphi,\theta,\eta)
such that

(T\rho)(\mu,\varphi,\theta)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho(\mu,\varphi,\eta)K(\mu,\varphi,\theta,\eta)d\eta.

We shall need the following lemma whose proof can be found in
[
47
, Lem. 4.4]
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
(\gamma,q,d,s_{0},s)
satisfy (
1.11
), then the following assertions hold true.

(i)

Let
T
be a multiplication operator by a real-valued function
M
, then the following holds true.

•

If
M(\mu,-\varphi,-\theta)=M(\mu,\varphi,\theta)
, then
T
is real and reversibility preserving Toeplitz in time and space operator.

•

If
M(\mu,-\varphi,-\theta)=-M(\mu,\varphi,\theta)
, then
T
is real and reversible Toeplitz in time and space operator.

Moreover,

\|T\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|M\|_{q,s+s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(ii)

Let
T
be an integral operator with a real-valued kernel
K
.

•

If
K(\mu,-\varphi,-\theta,-\eta)=K(\mu,\varphi,\theta,\eta)
, then
T
is a real and reversibility preserving Toeplitz in time operator.

•

If
K(\mu,-\varphi,-\theta,-\eta)=-K(\mu,\varphi,\theta,\eta)
, then
T
is a real and reversible Toeplitz in time operator.

In addition,

\displaystyle\|T\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{T}}\|K(\ast,\cdot,\centerdot,\eta+\centerdot)\|_{q,s+s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}d\eta\lesssim\|K\|_{q,H^{s+s_{0}}_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

and

\displaystyle\|T\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\rho\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\|K(\ast,\cdot,\centerdot,\eta+\centerdot)\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}d\eta+\|\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\|K(\ast,\cdot,\centerdot,\eta+\centerdot)\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}d\eta

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\rho\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|K\|_{q,H^{s}_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|K\|_{q,H^{s_{0}}_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

where the notation
\ast,\cdot,\centerdot
denote
\mu,\varphi,\theta
, respectively.

In the following lemma we shall study the action of a change of variables as in (
4.7
) on an integral operator. More precisely, we shall need two partial change of coordinates
\mathcal{B}^{1}
and
\mathcal{B}^{2}
acting respectively on the variables
\theta
and
\eta
and defined through

\displaystyle(\mathcal{B}^{1}\rho)(\mu,\varphi,\theta,\eta)

\displaystyle=\rho\big(\mu,\varphi,\theta+\beta_{1}(\mu,\varphi,\theta),\eta\big),

(4.18)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{B}^{2}\rho)(\mu,\varphi,\theta,\eta)

\displaystyle=\rho\big(\mu,\varphi,\theta,\eta+\beta_{2}(\mu,\varphi,\eta)\big),

with
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
two smooth functions satisfying (
4.9
).
A similar result is proved in
[
16
, Lem. 2.34]
for pseudo-differential integral operators, so we omit the proof here. We also include the difference estimate which is useful to study the stability of the Cantor sets in Section
7.2
. The proof of the difference estimate is standard and we shall also skip it here.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
(\gamma,q,d,s_{0},s)
satisfy (
1.11
). Given
r\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{s})
, we consider a
C^{\infty}
function in the form

K:(\mu,\varphi,\theta,\eta)\mapsto K(\mu,\varphi,\theta,\eta).

We consider the integral operator associated to
K
, namely

T\rho(\mu,\varphi,\theta)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho(\varphi,\eta)K(\mu,\varphi,\theta,\eta)d\eta.

Then the following assertions hold true.

(i)

Let
\mathcal{B}^{1}
and
\mathcal{B}^{2}
as in (
4.18
) associated to
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2},
respectively and enjoying the smallness condition (
4.9
). Then,

\|\mathcal{B}^{1}\mathcal{B}^{2}K\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|K\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\Big(\max_{i\in\{1,2\}}\|\beta_{i}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\Big)\|K\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s_{0}}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(4.19)

Now, assume that
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}=\beta
satisfies the following symmetry conditions

\displaystyle\beta(\mu,-\varphi,-\theta)=-\beta(\mu,\varphi,\theta).

(4.20)

Consider
\mathscr{B}
,
\mathcal{B}
be quasi-periodic changes of variables as in (
4.6
)-(
4.7
),
then

•

if
K(\mu,-\varphi,-\theta,-\eta)=K(\mu,\varphi,\theta,\eta)
, then
\mathcal{B}^{-1}T\mathscr{B}
is a real and reversibility preserving Toeplitz in time integral operator.

•

if
K(\mu,-\varphi,-\theta,-\eta)=-K(\mu,\varphi,\theta,\eta)
, then
\mathcal{B}^{-1}T\mathscr{B}
is a real and reversible Toeplitz in time inegral operator.

In this case, for any
k\in\mathbb{N},

\|\partial_{\theta}^{k}\mathcal{B}^{-1}T\mathscr{B}\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|K\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s+s_{0}+k}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\beta\|_{q,s+s_{0}+k}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|K\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s_{0}}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(4.21)

(ii)

Introduce
\mathcal{B}_{r}
a quasi-periodic change of variables as in (
4.7
) associated to
\beta_{r}
(linked to
r
) Consider
r_{1},\,r_{2}\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{s}).
Denote

\Delta_{12}r=r_{1}-r_{2},\hskip 10.00002pt\Delta_{12}f_{r}=f_{r_{1}}-f_{r_{2}}

for any quantity
f_{r}
depending on
r
and assume that there exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0
small enough such that

\forall i\in\{1,2\},\hskip 10.00002pt\|\beta_{r_{i}}\|_{q,2s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|K_{r_{i}}\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s_{0}+1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}.

(4.22)

Then, for any
k\in\mathbb{N},
the following estimate holds

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{12}\partial_{\theta}^{k}\mathcal{B}_{r}^{-1}T_{r}\mathscr{B}_{r}

\displaystyle\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s+s_{0}+k}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\Delta_{12}K_{r}\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s+s_{0}+k}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\Delta_{12}\beta_{r}\|_{q,s+s_{0}+k}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

(4.23)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\Big(\max_{i\in\{1,2\}}\|\beta_{r_{i}}\|_{q,s+s_{0}+k}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\Big)\|\Delta_{12}K_{r}\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s_{0}}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\Big(\max_{i\in\{1,2\}}\|K_{r_{i}}\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s+s_{0}+k+1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\max_{i\in\{1,2\}}\|\beta_{r_{i}}\|_{q,s+s_{0}+k+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\Big)\|\Delta_{12}\beta_{r}\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

5
Functional of interest and regularity aspects

The main goal of this section is to reformulate the problem in a dynamical system language more adapted to KAM techniques.
More precisely, we shall write the equation (
2.17
) as a Hamiltonian perturbation of an integrable system, given by the linear dynamics at the equilibrium state. Then, by
selecting finitely-many tangential sites and decomposing the phase space into tangential
and normal subspaces we can introduce action-angle variables on the tangential part allowing to reformulate the problem in terms of embedded tori. This reduces the problem into the search for zeros of a functional
\mathcal{F}
to which the Nash-Moser implicit function theorem will be applied. We shall also study in this section some regularity aspects for the perturbed Hamiltonian vector field appearing in
\mathcal{F}
and
needed during the Nash-Moser scheme. This approach has been intensively used
before, for instance in
[
3
,
5
,
13
,
14
,
16
]
.

Notice that, according to Lemmata
2.1
and
3.2
, the equation (
2.17
), that is also (
2.5
), can be written in the form

\partial_{t}r=\partial_{\theta}\mathrm{L}(b)(r)+X_{P}(r)\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with}\hskip 10.00002ptX_{P}(r):=\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{\theta}r+\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{K}_{b}\ast r-F_{b}[r],

(5.1)

where the nonlinear functional
F_{b}[r]
is introduced in (
2.6
) and the convolution kernel is given by (
3.10
). Since we shall look for small amplitude quasi-periodic solutions then it is more convenient to
rescale the solution as follows
r\mapsto\varepsilon r
with
r
bounded. Hence, the Hamiltonian equation (
2.17
) takes the form

\partial_{t}r=\partial_{\theta}\mathrm{L}(b)(r)+\varepsilon X_{P_{\varepsilon}}(r),

(5.2)

where
X_{P_{\varepsilon}}
is the Hamiltonian vector field defined by

X_{P_{\varepsilon}}(r):=\varepsilon^{-2}X_{P}(\varepsilon r).

Notice that (
5.2
) is the Hamiltonian system generated by
the rescaled Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}(r)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-2}H(\varepsilon r)

\displaystyle:=H_{\mathrm{L}}(r)+\varepsilon P_{\varepsilon}(r),

(5.3)

with
H_{\mathrm{L}}
the quadratic Hamiltonian defined in Lemma
3.2
and

\varepsilon P_{\varepsilon}(r)
containing terms
of higher order more than cubic.

5.1
Reformulation with the action-angle and normal variables

Recall from (
3.27
) that the tangential sites are defined by

\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathbb{S}}:=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{d}\}\subset\mathbb{N}^{*}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with}\hskip 10.00002pt1\leqslant j_{1}<j_{2}<\ldots<j_{d}.

We now define the symmetrized tangential sets
\overline{\mathbb{S}}
and
\mathbb{S}_{0}
by

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{S}}:={\mathbb{S}}\cup(-{\mathbb{S}})=\{\pm j,\,\,j\in{\mathbb{S}}\}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathbb{S}}_{0}=\overline{\mathbb{S}}\cup\{0\}.

(5.4)

Then we decompose the phase space
L^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T})
into the following
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
-orthogonal direct sum

L^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T})=L_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}}\overset{\perp}{\oplus}{L}^{2}_{\bot},\hskip 10.00002ptL_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}}:=\Big\{\sum_{j\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}}r_{j}e_{j},\,\,\overline{r_{j}}=r_{-j}\Big\},\hskip 10.00002ptL_{\bot}^{2}:=\Big\{z=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\mathbb{S}_{0}}z_{j}e_{j}\in L^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T})\Big\}\,,

(5.5)

where we denote
e_{j}(\theta)=e^{{\rm i}j\theta}.
The associated orthogonal projectors
\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}},\Pi^{\bot}_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}
are defined by

r=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}r_{j}e_{j}=v+z,\hskip 10.00002ptv:=\Pi_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}}r:=\sum_{j\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}}r_{j}e_{j},\hskip 10.00002ptz:=\Pi^{\bot}_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}r:=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\mathbb{S}_{0}}r_{j}e_{j},

(5.6)

where
v
and
z
are respectively called the tangential and normal variables. For fixed small amplitudes
(\mathtt{a}_{j})_{j\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}}\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*})^{d}
satisfying
\mathtt{a}_{-j}=\mathtt{a}_{j}
, we introduce the action-angle variables on the tangential set
L_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}}
by making the following symplectic polar change of coordinates

\forall j\in\overline{\mathbb{S}},\hskip 10.00002ptr_{j}=\sqrt{\mathtt{a}_{j}^{2}+{|j|}I_{j}}\,e^{{\rm i}\vartheta_{j}},

(5.7)

where

\forall j\in\overline{\mathbb{S}},\hskip 10.00002ptI_{-j}=I_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\vartheta_{-j}=-\vartheta_{j}\in\mathbb{T}.

(5.8)

Thus, any function
r

of the phase space
L_{0}^{2}
can be represented as

r=A(\vartheta,I,z):=v(\vartheta,I)+z\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{where}\hskip 10.00002ptv(\vartheta,I):=\sum_{j\in\overline{\mathbb{S}}}\sqrt{\mathtt{a}_{j}^{2}+{|j|}I_{j}}\,e^{{\rm i}\vartheta_{j}}e_{j}\,.

(5.9)

Observe that the function
v(-{\omega}_{\textnormal{Eq}}(b)t,0)
, where
{\omega}_{\textnormal{Eq}}
is defined in (
3.28
), corresponds to the solution of the linear system (
3.8
) described by (
3.42
). In these new coordinates, the involution
{\mathscr{S}}
defined in (
2.22
) reads

{\mathfrak{S}}:(\vartheta,I,z)\mapsto(-\vartheta,I,{\mathscr{S}}z)

(5.10)

and the symplectic
2
-form in (
2.20
) becomes, after straightforward computations using (
5.7
) and (
5.8
),

{\mathcal{W}}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{S}}d\vartheta_{j}\wedge dI_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\mathbb{S}_{0}}\frac{1}{{\rm i}j}dr_{j}\wedge dr_{-j}=\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{S}}d\vartheta_{j}\wedge dI_{j}\Big)\oplus{\mathcal{W}}_{|L_{\bot}^{2}},

(5.11)

where
{\mathcal{W}}_{|L^{2}_{\bot}}
denotes the restriction of
\mathcal{W}
to
L^{2}_{\bot}
.
This proves that the transformation
A
defined in (
5.9
) is symplectic and in the action-angle and normal coordinates
(\vartheta,I,z)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times{L}^{2}_{\bot}
, the Hamiltonian system generated by
{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}
in (
5
) transforms into the one generated
by the Hamiltonian

H_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\circ A.

(5.12)

Since
\mathrm{L}(b)
in Lemma
3.2
preserves the subspaces
L_{\overline{\mathbb{S}}}
and
{L}^{2}_{\bot}
then the quadratic Hamiltonian
H_{\rm L}
in (
3.13
) (see (
3.14
))
in the variables
(\vartheta,I,z)
reads, up to an additive constant,

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{L}}\circ A=-\sum_{j\in\mathbb{S}}\,\Omega_{j}(b)I_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathrm{L}(b)\,z,z\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}=-{\omega}_{\textnormal{Eq}}(b)\cdot I+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathrm{L}(b)\,z,z\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})},

(5.13)

where
{\omega}_{\textnormal{Eq}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the unperturbed
tangential frequency vector defined by (
3.28
).
By (
5
) and (
5.13
),
the Hamiltonian
H_{\varepsilon}
in (
5.12
) reads

H_{\varepsilon}={\mathcal{N}}+\varepsilon\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm with}\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathcal{N}}:=-{\omega}_{\textnormal{Eq}}(b)\cdot I+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathrm{L}(b)\,z,z\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}:=P_{\varepsilon}\circ A.

(5.14)

We look for an embedded invariant torus

i:\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathbb{T}^{d}&\rightarrow&\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times{L}^{2}_{\bot}\\
\varphi&\mapsto&i(\varphi):=(\vartheta(\varphi),I(\varphi),z(\varphi))\end{array}

(5.15)

of the Hamiltonian vector field

X_{H_{\varepsilon}}:=(\partial_{I}H_{\varepsilon},-\partial_{\vartheta}H_{\varepsilon},\Pi_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}^{\bot}\partial_{\theta}\nabla_{z}H_{\varepsilon})

(5.16)

filled by quasi-periodic solutions with Diophantine frequency
vector
\omega
.
We point out that for the value
\varepsilon=0
the Hamiltonian system

\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}i(\varphi)=X_{H_{0}}(i(\varphi))

possesses, for any value of the parameter
b\in(b_{0},b_{1})
, the invariant torus

i_{\textnormal{\tiny{flat}}}(\varphi):=(\varphi,0,0).

(5.17)

Now we consider the family of Hamiltonians,

\displaystyle H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}:={\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}+\varepsilon{\mathcal{P}}_{\varepsilon}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{where}\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathcal{N}}_{\alpha}:=\alpha\cdot I+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathrm{L}(b)\,z,z\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})},

(5.18)

which depends on the constant vector
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For the value
\alpha=-{\omega}_{\textnormal{Eq}}(b)
we have
H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}=H_{\varepsilon}
.
The parameter
\alpha
is introduced in order to ensure the validity of some compatibility conditions during the approximate inverse process.
We look for zeros of the nonlinear operator

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{F}(i,\alpha,(b,\omega),\varepsilon):=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}i(\varphi)-X_{H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}}(i(\varphi))\\
\mbox{\hskip 56.9055pt}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\vartheta(\varphi)-\alpha-\varepsilon\partial_{I}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}(i(\varphi))\\
\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}I(\varphi)+\varepsilon\partial_{\vartheta}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}(i(\varphi))\\
\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}z(\varphi)-\partial_{\theta}\big[\mathrm{L}(b)z(\varphi)+\varepsilon\nabla_{z}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}\big(i(\varphi)\big)\big]\end{array}\right),\end{array}

(5.19)

where
\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}
is defined in (
5
). For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
the Hamiltonian
H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}
is invariant under the involution
\mathfrak{S}
defined in (
5.10
),

H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}\circ\mathfrak{S}=H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}.

Thus, we look for reversible solutions of
\mathcal{F}(i,\alpha,(b,\omega),\varepsilon)=0,
namely satisfying

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\vartheta(-\varphi)=-\vartheta(\varphi),&I(-\varphi)=I(\varphi),&z(-\varphi)=(\mathscr{S}z)(\varphi).\end{array}

(5.20)

We define the periodic component
\mathfrak{I}
of the torus
i
by

\mathfrak{I}(\varphi):=i(\varphi)-(\varphi,0,0)=(\Theta(\varphi),I(\varphi),z(\varphi))\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ with }\hskip 10.00002pt\Theta(\varphi)=\vartheta(\varphi)-\varphi,

and the weighted Sobolev norm of
\mathfrak{I}
as

\|\mathfrak{I}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}:=\|\Theta\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|I\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|z\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

5.2
Regularity of the perturbed Hamiltonian vector field

This section is devoted to some regularity aspects of the Hamiltonian involved in the equation (
2.17
).
We shall need the following lemma.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
(\gamma,q,s_{0},s)
satisfy (
1.11
). There exists
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1]
such that if

\|r\|_{q,s_{0}+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\leqslant\varepsilon_{0},

then the operators
\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{L}_{r}
and
\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{S}_{r}
, defined in (
3.2
) and (
3.3
) write

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{L}_{r}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{K}_{1,b}\ast\cdot+\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{L}_{r,1}\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm with}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{L}_{r,1}(\rho)(b,\varphi,\theta):=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho(\varphi,\eta)\mathbb{K}_{r,1}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)d\eta,

(5.21)

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{S}_{r}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{K}_{2,b}\ast\cdot+\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{S}_{r,1}\;\;\;\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{S}_{r,1}(\rho)(b,\varphi,\theta)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho(\varphi,\eta)\mathscr{K}_{r,1}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)d\eta

(5.22)

where
\mathcal{K}_{1,b}
,
\mathcal{K}_{2,b}
are given by (
3.11
)-(
3.12
) and the kernels
\mathbb{K}_{r,1}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta),\mathscr{K}_{r,1}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy the following symmetry property: if
r(-\varphi,-\theta)=r(\varphi,\theta)
then

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}_{r,1}(b,-\varphi,-\theta,-\eta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{K}_{r,1}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta),

(5.23)

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}_{r,1}(b,-\varphi,-\theta,-\eta)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{K}_{r,1}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)

(5.24)

and the following estimates

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{K}_{r,1}\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|r\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}},

(5.25)

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{K}_{r,1}\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|r\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(5.26)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{K}_{1,b}\ast\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}},

(5.27)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{K}_{2,b}\ast\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}},

(5.28)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{L}_{r,1}\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|r\|_{q,s_{0}+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|r\|_{q,s+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}},

(5.29)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{S}_{r,1}\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|r\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|r\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(5.30)

Proof.

According to (
3.1
) we may write

\displaystyle A_{r}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)

\displaystyle=2b\left|\sin\left(\tfrac{\eta-\theta}{2}\right)\right|\bigg(\bigg(\frac{R(b,\varphi,\eta)-R(b,\varphi,\theta)}{2b\sin\big(\tfrac{\eta-\theta}{2}\big)}\bigg)^{2}+\frac{1}{b^{2}}R(b,\varphi,\eta)R(b,\varphi,\theta)\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle:=2b\left|\sin\left(\tfrac{\eta-\theta}{2}\right)\right|v_{r,1}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta).

(5.31)

Notice that
v_{r,1}
is smooth when
r
is smooth and small enough, and
v_{0,1}=1.
More precisely, by using Lemma
4.1
-
(iv)
-
(v)
combined with Lemma
4.2
-
(ii)
and the smallness condition on
r
, we get

\displaystyle\|v_{r,1}-1\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|r\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(5.32)

Using the morphism property of the logarithm, we can write

\displaystyle\log(A_{r}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta))

\displaystyle=\log\big(2b\big)+\tfrac{1}{2}\log\left(\sin^{2}\left(\tfrac{\eta-\theta}{2}\right)\right)+\log\left(v_{r,1}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)\right)

\displaystyle:=\log\big(2b\big)+\mathcal{K}_{1,b}(\eta-\theta)+\mathbb{K}_{r,1}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)

(5.33)

and (
5.23
) immediately follows. Moreover, (
3.2
) and (
5.33
) give (
5.21
).
Applying Lemma
4.1
-(v) together with (
5.32
) and the smallness condition on
r
, we obtain (
5.25
). Using (
5.25
), Lemma
4.7
-
(ii)
and the smallness property on
r
, we get (
5.29
).
Similarly, from (
3.18
) we can link
B_{r}^{2}
to
B_{0}^{2}
by

\displaystyle B_{r}^{2}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)

\displaystyle=B_{0}^{2}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)+\Big(R^{2}(b,\varphi,\theta)R^{2}(b,\varphi,\eta)-b^{4}\Big)-2\Big(R(b,\varphi,\theta)R(b,\varphi,\eta)-b^{2}\Big)\cos(\eta-\theta)

\displaystyle=B_{0}^{2}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)\big(1+P_{r}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)\big)

with

P_{r}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta):=\tfrac{\big(R^{2}(b,\varphi,\theta)R^{2}(b,\varphi,\eta)-b^{4}\big)-2\big(R(b,\varphi,\theta)R(b,\varphi,\eta)-b^{2}\big)\cos(\eta-\theta)}{1+b^{4}-2b^{2}\cos(\eta-\theta)}.

so that we can write

\displaystyle\log\big(B_{r}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)\big)

\displaystyle=\log\big(B_{0}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)\big)+\tfrac{1}{2}\log\big(1+P_{r}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)\big)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{K}_{2,b}(\eta-\theta)+\mathscr{K}_{r,1}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)

(5.34)

and (
5.24
) immediately follows. Moreover, (
3.3
) and (
5.2
) give (
5.22
).
Notice that that
P_{r}
is smooth with respect to each variable and with respect to
r
with
P_{0}=0
. We conclude by Lemma
4.1
-(iv)-(v) and the smallness property on
r
that

\|P_{r}\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|r\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

As a consequence, composition laws in Lemma
4.1
together with the smallness property on
r
imply (
5.26
).
Then, using (
5.26
), Lemma
4.7
-
(ii)
and the smallness property on
r
, we get (
5.30
). The estimates (
5.27
)-(
5.28
) can be obtained using (
3.22
), (
3.24
) and Leibniz rule combined with the following estimate

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\|b\mapsto b^{n}\|_{q}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim 1.

This ends the proof of Lemma
5.1
.
∎

We now provide tame estimates for the vector field
X_{P}
defined in (
5.1
).

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
(\gamma,q,s_{0},s)
satisfy (
1.11
). There exists
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1]
such that if

\|r\|_{q,s_{0}+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\leqslant\varepsilon_{0},

then the vector field
X_{P}
, defined in (
5.1
) satisfies the following estimates

(i)

\|X_{P}(r)\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|r\|_{q,s+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|r\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(ii)

\|d_{r}X_{P}(r)[\rho]\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\rho\|_{q,s+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|r\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|r\|_{q,s+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(iii)

\|d_{r}^{2}X_{P}(r)[\rho_{1},\rho_{2}]\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\rho_{1}\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\big(\|\rho_{1}\|_{q,s+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|r\|_{q,s+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{1}\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\big)\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}
.

Proof.

We shall follow the proof developed in
[
15
, Lem 10.2]
. We first prove the estimate
(iii)
and
the estimates
(ii)
and
(i)
then follow by Taylor formula since
d_{r}X_{P}(0)=0
and
X_{P}(0)=0
.
Recall from Lemma
3.1
, (
5.21
) and (
5.22
) that

d_{r}X_{H}(r)[\rho]=-d_{r}F_{b}(r)[\rho]=-\partial_{\theta}\left(V_{r}\rho\right)-\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{K}_{b}\ast\rho-\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{L}_{r,1}\rho+\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{S}_{r,1}\rho.

According to (
5.1
),
P
is is the Hamiltonian generated by higher order more than cubic terms
H_{\geqslant 3}
. Then differentiating with respect to
r
the last expression we obtain

d_{r}^{2}X_{P}(r)[\rho_{1},\rho_{2}]=-\partial_{\theta}\left(\big(d_{r}V_{r}[\rho_{2}]\big)\rho_{1}\right)-\partial_{\theta}\left(d_{r}\mathbf{L}_{r,1}[\rho_{2}]\rho_{1}\right)+\partial_{\theta}\left(d_{r}\mathbf{S}_{r,1}[\rho_{2}]\rho_{1}\right).

(5.35)

Recall, from (
5.21
) and (
5.33
), that

\mathbf{L}_{r,1}(\rho)(b,\varphi,\theta)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho(\varphi,\eta)\log(v_{r,1}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta))d\eta.

(5.36)

Hence by differentiation we obtain

\displaystyle d_{r}\mathbf{L}_{r,1}(r)[\rho_{2}]\rho_{1}(b,\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{1}(\varphi,\eta)\tfrac{\big(d_{r}v_{r,1}^{2}\big)[\rho_{2}](b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)}{v_{r,1}^{2}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)}d\eta.

(5.37)

Coming back to (
5.2
) it is obvious that the dependance in
r
of the functional
v_{r,1}^{2}
is smooth. Straightforward calculus leads to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}d_{r}v_{r,1}^{2}(r)[\rho_{2}](b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{R(b,\varphi,\theta)-R(b,\varphi,\eta)}{\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\eta-\theta}{2}\right)}\left(\tfrac{\rho_{2}(\varphi,\theta)}{R(b,\varphi,\theta)}-\tfrac{\rho_{2}(\varphi,\eta)}{R(b,\varphi,\eta)}\right)+\tfrac{\rho_{2}(\varphi,\theta)R^{2}(b,\varphi,\eta)+\rho_{2}(\varphi,\eta)R^{2}(b,\varphi,\theta)}{R(b,\varphi,\theta)R(b,\varphi,\eta)}.

Using (
5.32
) combined with the law products stated in Lemma
4.1
, Lemma
4.2
-
(ii)
and the smallness condition of Lemma
5.2
we find that

\|d_{r}v_{r,1}^{2}(r)[\rho_{2}]\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|r\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(5.38)

According to (
5.38
), (
5.37
) and using Lemma
4.1
-
(iv)
-
(v)
, Lemma
4.7
-
(ii)
and the smallness condition we obtain,

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\theta}d_{r}\mathbf{L}_{r,1}(r)[\rho_{2}]\rho_{1}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\|d_{r}\mathbf{L}_{r,1}(r)[\rho_{2}]\rho_{1}\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\rho_{1}\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\rho_{1}\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\big(\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|r\|_{q,s+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\big).

(5.39)

Now we shall move to the estimate of
d_{r}\mathbf{S}_{r,1}(r)[\rho_{2}]\rho_{1}(b,\varphi,\theta).

By differentiating with respect to
r
in (
5.22
) and (
5.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle d_{r}\mathbf{S}_{r,1}(r)[\rho_{2}]\rho_{1}(b,\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{1}(\varphi,\eta)\tfrac{\big(d_{r}B_{r}^{2}\big)[\rho_{2}](b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)}{B_{r}^{2}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)}d\eta.

In view of (
3.18
), direct computations yield

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}d_{r}B_{r}^{2}(r)[\rho_{2}](b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)

\displaystyle=\rho_{2}(\varphi,\theta)R^{2}(b,\varphi,\eta)+\rho_{2}(\varphi,\eta)R^{2}(b,\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt-\Big(\rho_{2}(\varphi,\theta)\tfrac{R(b,\varphi,\eta)}{R(b,\varphi,\theta)}+\rho_{2}(\varphi,\eta)\tfrac{R(b,\varphi,\theta)}{R(b,\varphi,\eta)}\Big)\cos(\eta-\theta).

Then, Lemma
4.1
-
(iv)
-
(v)
and the smallness condition on
r
imply

\displaystyle\|d_{r}B_{r}^{2}(r)[\rho_{2}]\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|r\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

It follows from Lemma
4.7
-
(ii)
, that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\theta}d_{r}\mathbf{S}_{r,1}(r)[\rho_{2}]\rho_{1}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\|d_{r}\mathbf{S}_{r,1}(r)[\rho_{2}]\rho_{1}\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\rho_{1}\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\rho_{1}\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\big(\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|r\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\big).

(5.40)

Next we shall move to the estimate of
d_{r}V_{r}[\rho_{2}]
. From Lemma
3.1
, we can write

\displaystyle V_{r}=V_{r}^{0}+V_{r}^{1}+V_{r}^{2},\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with}\hskip 10.00002ptV_{r}^{0}(b,\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle:=-\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\tfrac{R^{2}(b,\varphi,\eta)}{R^{2}(b,\varphi,\theta)}d\eta,

\displaystyle V_{r}^{1}(b,\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle:=-\tfrac{1}{R(b,\varphi,\theta)}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log\big(A_{r}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)\big)\partial_{\eta}\big(R(b,\varphi,\eta)\sin(\eta-\theta)\big)d\eta,

\displaystyle V_{r}^{2}(b,\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle:=-\tfrac{1}{R^{3}(b,\varphi,\theta)}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log\big(B_{r}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)\big)\partial_{\eta}\big(R(b,\varphi,\eta)\sin(\eta-\theta)\big)d\eta.

Differentiating
V_{r}^{0}
with respect to
r
in the direction
\rho_{2}
yields

d_{r}V_{r}^{0}(r)[\rho_{2}](\theta)=-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\tfrac{\rho_{2}(\varphi,\theta)R^{2}(b,\varphi,\eta)-\rho_{2}(\varphi,\eta)R^{2}(b,\varphi,\theta)}{R^{4}(b,\varphi,\theta)}d\eta.

Law products in Lemma
4.1
and the smallness condition in
r
then imply

\|d_{r}V_{r}^{0}(r)[\rho_{2}]\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|r\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(5.41)

Differentiating
V_{r}^{1}
with respect to
r
in the direction
\rho_{2}
gives

\displaystyle d_{r}V_{r}^{1}(r)[\rho_{2}](\theta)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log\left(A_{r}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)\right)\partial_{\eta}d_{r}f_{r}[\rho_{2}](b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\tfrac{\big(d_{r}v_{r,1}^{2}\big)[\rho_{2}](b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)}{v_{r,1}^{2}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)}\partial_{\eta}f_{r}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{I}_{1}(\theta)+\mathcal{I}_{2}(\theta),

with

\displaystyle f_{r}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)

\displaystyle:=\tfrac{R(b,\varphi,\eta)}{R(b,\varphi,\theta)}\sin(\eta-\theta).

Straightforward computations give

d_{r}f_{r}[\rho_{2}](b,\varphi,\theta)=\tfrac{\rho_{2}(\varphi,\eta)R^{2}(b,\varphi,\theta)-\rho_{2}(\varphi,\theta)R^{2}(b,\varphi,\eta)}{R^{3}(b,\varphi,\theta)R(b,\varphi,\eta)}\sin(\eta-\theta).

Then, by law products and composition laws in Lemma
4.1
we immediately deduce that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\eta}{f}_{r}\|_{q,H^{s}_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\|r\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}},

(5.42)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\eta}d_{r}f_{r}[\rho_{2}]\|_{q,H^{s}_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(1+\|r\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\big)\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\big(1+\|r\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\big)\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(5.43)

The following estimate on
\mathcal{I}_{2}
can be obtained combining (
5.38
), (
5.32
) and (
5.42
) together with Lemma
4.1
-
(iv)
-
(v)
and the smallness property on
r
.

\|\mathcal{I}_{2}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|r\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(5.44)

As for
\mathcal{I}_{1}
we argue in a similar way to Lemma
5.1
to get

\|\mathcal{I}_{1}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|r\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(5.45)

Putting together (
5.44
) and (
5.45
) yields

\|d_{r}V_{r}^{1}(r)[\rho_{2}]\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|r\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(5.46)

Differentiating
V_{r}^{2}
with respect to
r
in the direction
\rho_{2}
yields

\displaystyle d_{r}V_{r}^{2}(r)[\rho_{2}](b,\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle=-\mathop{\text{\Large$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}}$}}\nolimits\log\big(B_{r}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)\big)\partial_{\eta}\left(\tfrac{\rho_{2}(\varphi,\eta)R^{2}(b,\varphi,\theta)-3\rho_{2}(\varphi,\theta)R^{2}(b,\varphi,\eta)}{R^{5}(b,\varphi,\theta)R(b,\varphi,\eta)}\sin(\eta-\theta)\right)d\eta

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt-\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\text{\Large$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\mathbb{T}$}}$}}\nolimits\tfrac{\big(d_{r}B_{r}^{2}\big)[\rho_{2}](b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)}{B_{r}^{2}(b,\varphi,\theta,\eta)}\partial_{\eta}\Big(\tfrac{R(b,\varphi,\eta)}{R^{3}(b,\varphi,\theta)}\sin(\eta-\theta)\Big)d\eta.

Arguing in a similar way as above we find

\|d_{r}V_{r}^{2}(r)[\rho_{2}]\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|r\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(5.47)

Putting together (
5.41
), (
5.46
) and (
5.47
) gives

\|d_{r}V_{r}(r)[\rho_{2}]\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|r\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(5.48)

Therefore, according to the law products in Lemma
4.1
, (
5.48
) and the smallness condition we obtain

\displaystyle\big\|\partial_{\theta}\big(d_{r}V_{r}(r)[\rho_{2}]\rho_{1}\big)\big\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|d_{r}V_{r}(r)[\rho_{2}]\rho_{1}\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\|d_{r}V_{r}(r)[\rho_{2}]\big\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\big\|\rho_{1}\big\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\big\|d_{r}V_{r}(r)[\rho_{2}]\big\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\big\|\rho_{1}\big\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\rho_{1}\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|r\|_{q,s+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{1}\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\rho_{1}\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

Combining the latter estimate with (
5.35
), (
5.2
) and (
5.2
) allows to get

\|d_{r}^{2}X_{P}(r)[\rho_{1},\rho_{2}]\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\rho_{1}\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|r\|_{q,s+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{1}\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\rho_{1}\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho_{2}\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

Using Sobolev embeddings we get the desired result. This concludes the proof of Lemma
5.2
.
∎

Notice in particular that Lemma
5.2
-(i) implies that there is no singlarity in
\varepsilon
for the rescaled vector field
X_{P_{\varepsilon}}
defined in (
5.2
).
Based on the previous lemma we obtain tame estimates for the Hamiltonian vector field

X_{\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}}=(\partial_{I}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon},-\partial_{\vartheta}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon},\Pi_{\mathbb{S}}^{\perp}\partial_{\theta}\nabla_{z}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon})

defined by (
5.14
) and (
5.16
). The proof can be done in a similar way to
[
16
, Lem. 5.1]
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
(\gamma,q,s_{0},s)
satisfy (
1.11
).
There exists
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
such that if

\varepsilon\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\|\mathfrak{I}\|_{q,s_{0}+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\leqslant 1,

then the perturbed Hamiltonian vector field
X_{\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}}
satisfies the following tame estimates,

(i)

\|X_{\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}}(i)\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim 1+\|\mathfrak{I}\|_{q,s+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(ii)

\big\|d_{i}X_{\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}}(i)[\,\widehat{i}\,]\big\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\,\widehat{i}\,\|_{q,s+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\mathfrak{I}\|_{q,s+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\,\widehat{i}\,\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(iii)

\big\|d_{i}^{2}X_{\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}}(i)[\,\widehat{i},\widehat{i}\,]\big\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\,\widehat{i}\,\|_{q,s+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\,\widehat{i}\,\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\mathfrak{I}\|_{q,s+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\big(\|\,\widehat{i}\,\|_{q,s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\big)^{2}.

6
Construction of an approximate right inverse

In order to apply a modified Nash-Moser scheme, we need to construct an approximate right inverse
of the linearized operator associated to the functional
\mathcal{F}
, that is

d_{(i,\alpha)}{\mathcal{F}}(i_{0},\alpha_{0})[\widehat{\imath}\,,\widehat{\alpha}]=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\widehat{\imath}-d_{i}X_{H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha_{0}}}(i_{0}(\varphi))[\widehat{\imath}]-(\widehat{\alpha},0,0).

(6.1)

where
\mathcal{F}
is defined in (
5.19
),
\alpha_{0}:\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a vector-valued function and
i_{0}=(\vartheta_{0},I_{0},z_{0})
is an arbitrary torus close to the flat one and satisfying the reversibility condition

\vartheta_{0}(-\varphi)=-\vartheta_{0}(\varphi),\hskip 10.00002ptI_{0}(-\varphi)=I_{0}(\varphi)\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 10.00002ptz_{0}(-\varphi)=(\mathscr{S}z_{0})(\varphi).

(6.2)

For this aim, we may follow the procedure introduced in
[
11
]
and slightly simplified in
[
37
, Sec. 6]
. The main idea consists in conjugating (
6.1
) by a linear diffeomorphism of the toroidal phase space
\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times L^{2}_{\perp}
to a triangular system in the action-angles-normal variables up to small fast decaying error terms and terms vanishing at an exact solution. Then, to solve the triangular system we are led to almost invert the linearized operator in the normal directions, given by

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega}:=\Pi_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}^{\perp}\Big(\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-\partial_{\theta}(\partial_{z}\nabla_{z}H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha_{0}})(i_{0}(\varphi))-\varepsilon\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{R}(\varphi)\Big)\Pi_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}^{\perp},

(6.3)

where
H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha_{0}}
is given by (
5.18
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\varphi)

\displaystyle:=L_{2}^{\top}(\varphi)\partial_{I}\nabla_{I}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0}(\varphi))L_{2}(\varphi)+L_{2}^{\top}(\varphi)\partial_{z}\nabla_{I}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0}(\varphi))+\partial_{I}\nabla_{z}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0}(\varphi))L_{2}(\varphi),

(6.4)

\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}
is defined by (
5.14
) and

\displaystyle L_{2}(\phi)

\displaystyle:=-[(\partial_{\vartheta}\widetilde{z}_{0})(\vartheta_{0}(\phi))]^{\top}\partial_{\theta}^{-1},\hskip 10.00002pt\widetilde{z}_{0}(\vartheta)

\displaystyle:=z_{0}(\vartheta_{0}^{-1}(\vartheta)).

(6.5)

Here, for any linear operator
A\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},L^{2}_{\perp})
the transposed operator
A^{\top}:L^{2}_{\perp}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined through the duality relation

\displaystyle\forall\,u\in L^{2}_{\perp}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\hskip 10.00002pt\big\langle A^{\top}u,v\big\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}=\big\langle u,Av\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

(6.6)

We point out the presence of the remainder term due to the linear change of variables performed to decouple the dynamics of the action-angle components from the normal ones. For more details we refer the reader to
[
37
, Sec. 6]
.

6.1
Linearized operator in the normal direction

Our main goal here is to explore the structure of the linear operator
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega}
, introduced in (
6.3
). We have the following result.
The following lemma describes the asymptotic structure of
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega}

around the equilibrium state, described in Lemma
3.1
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
(\gamma,q,d,s_{0})
satisfy (
1.11
).
Then,
the operator
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega}
defined in (
6.3
) takes the form

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega}=\Pi_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}^{\perp}\Big(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon r}-\varepsilon\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{R}\Big)\Pi_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}^{\perp},

(6.7)

where

(i)

the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon r}
is given by

\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon r}:=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}+\partial_{\theta}\big(V_{\varepsilon r}\cdot\big)+\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{L}_{\varepsilon r}-\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{S}_{\varepsilon r},

(6.8)

with
V_{\varepsilon r},

\mathbf{L}_{\varepsilon r}
and
\mathbf{S}_{\varepsilon r}
defined by (
3.1
), (
3.2
) and (
3.3
).

(ii)

the function
r
is given by

r(\varphi,\cdot)=A(i_{0}(\varphi)),

(6.9)

satisfies the following symmetry property

r(-\varphi,-\theta)=r(\varphi,\theta)

(6.10)

and the following estimates

\displaystyle\|r\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\|\mathfrak{I}_{0}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}},

(6.11)

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{12}r\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Delta_{12}i\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\Delta_{12}i\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}\|\mathfrak{I}_{j}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(6.12)

(iii)

the operator
\mathcal{R}
, defined in (
6.4
), is an integral operator with kernel
J
satisfying the symmetry property

J(-\varphi,-\theta,-\eta)=J(\varphi,\theta,\eta)

(6.13)

and the following estimates: for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{T}}\|(\partial_{\theta}^{\ell}J)(\ast,\cdot,\centerdot,\eta+\centerdot)\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\|\mathfrak{I}_{0}\|_{q,s+3+\ell}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}},

(6.14)

\displaystyle\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{T}}\|\Delta_{12}(\partial_{\theta}^{\ell}J)(\ast,\cdot,\centerdot,\eta+\centerdot)\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Delta_{12}i\|_{q,s+3+\ell}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\Delta_{12}i\|_{q,s_{0}+3}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}\|\mathfrak{I}_{j}\|_{q,s+3+\ell}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(6.15)

where
*,\cdot,\centerdot,
denote successively the variables
b,\varphi,\theta
and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}_{j}(\varphi)=i_{j}(\varphi)-(\varphi,0,0).

Proof.

From (
5.18
), (
5.12
), (
5.9
) and (
5
) we obtain

\displaystyle(\partial_{z}\nabla_{z}H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha_{0}})(i_{0}(\varphi))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{L}(b)\Pi_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}^{\perp}+\varepsilon\partial_{z}\nabla_{z}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0}(\varphi))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{L}(b)\Pi_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}^{\perp}+\varepsilon\Pi_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}^{\perp}\partial_{r}\nabla_{r}P_{\varepsilon}(A(i_{0}(\varphi)))

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}^{\perp}\partial_{r}\nabla_{r}\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}(A(i_{0}(\varphi)))

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}^{\perp}\partial_{r}\nabla_{r}H(\varepsilon A(i_{0}(\varphi))).

According to the general form of the linearized operator stated in Lemma
3.1
one has

-\partial_{\theta}(\partial_{z}\nabla_{z}H_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha_{0}})(i_{0}(\varphi))=\Pi_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}^{\perp}\big(\partial_{\theta}\big(V_{\varepsilon r}(b,\varphi,\centerdot)\cdot\big)+\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{L}_{\varepsilon r}-\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{S}_{\varepsilon r}\big)\Pi_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}^{\perp}.

Inserting this identity into (
6.3
) gives (
6.7
).
The operator
\mathcal{R}(\varphi)
in (
6.4
) may be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\varphi)=\mathcal{R}_{1}(\varphi)+\mathcal{R}_{2}(\varphi)+\mathcal{R}_{3}(\varphi),\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm with}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{R}_{1}(\varphi)

\displaystyle:=L_{2}^{\top}(\varphi)\partial_{I}\nabla_{I}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0}(\varphi))L_{2}(\varphi),

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{2}(\varphi)

\displaystyle:=L_{2}^{\top}(\varphi)\partial_{z}\nabla_{I}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0}(\varphi)),

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{3}(\varphi)

\displaystyle:=\partial_{I}\nabla_{z}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0}(\varphi))L_{2}(\varphi).

Notice that
\mathcal{R}_{1}(\varphi),

\mathcal{R}_{2}(\varphi)
and
\mathcal{R}_{3}(\varphi)
have a finite-dimensional rank. In fact, from (
6.5
) and (
6.6
) one may write

L_{2}(\varphi)[\rho]=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\big\langle L_{2}(\varphi)[\rho],\underline{e}_{k}\big\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\underline{e}_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\big\langle\rho,L_{2}^{\top}(\varphi)[\underline{e}_{k}]\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\,\underline{e}_{k},

with
\displaystyle(\underline{e}_{k})_{k=1}^{d}
being the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Hence

\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1}(\varphi)[\rho]=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\big\langle\rho,L_{2}^{\top}(\varphi)[\underline{e}_{k}]\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}A_{1}(\varphi)[\underline{e}_{k}]&\quad\mbox{with }\quad A_{1}(\varphi)=L_{2}^{\top}(\varphi)\partial_{I}\nabla_{I}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0}(\varphi)),\\
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{3}(\varphi)[\rho]=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\big\langle\rho,L_{2}^{\top}(\varphi)[\underline{e}_{k}]\big\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}A_{3}(\varphi)[\underline{e}_{k}]&\quad\mbox{with }\quad A_{3}(\varphi)=\partial_{I}\nabla_{z}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0}(\varphi)).\end{array}

Analogously, since
A_{2}(\varphi):=\partial_{z}\nabla_{I}\mathcal{P}_{\varepsilon}(i_{0}(\varphi)):L^{2}_{\perp}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d},
then we may write

\mathcal{R}_{2}(\varphi)[\rho]=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\big\langle\rho,A_{2}^{\top}(\varphi)[\underline{e}_{k}]\big\rangle_{L^{2}\,(\mathbb{T})}L_{2}^{\top}(\varphi)[\underline{e}_{k}].

By setting

\displaystyle g_{k,1}(\varphi,\theta)=g_{k,3}(\varphi,\theta)=\chi_{k,2}(\varphi,\theta):=L_{2}^{\top}(\varphi)[\underline{e}_{k}](\theta),\hskip 20.00003ptg_{k,2}(\varphi,\theta):=A_{2}^{\top}(\varphi)[\underline{e}_{k}](\theta),

\displaystyle\chi_{k,1}(\varphi,\theta):=A_{1}(\varphi)[\underline{e}_{k}](\theta),\hskip 20.00003pt\chi_{k,3}(\varphi,\theta):=A_{3}(\varphi)[\underline{e}_{k}](\theta),

we can see that the operator
\mathcal{R}
takes the integral form

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\rho(\varphi,\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho(\varphi,\eta)J(\varphi,\theta,\eta)d\eta,\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with}\hskip 10.00002ptJ(\varphi,\theta,\eta):=\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{3}\sum_{k=1}^{d}g_{k,k^{\prime}}(\varphi,\eta)\chi_{k,k^{\prime}}(\varphi,\theta).

The symmetry property (
6.13
) is a consequence of the definition of
r
and the reversibility condition (
6.2
) imposed on the torus
i_{0}.
The estimates (
6.15
), (
6.14
), (
6.11
) and (
6.12
) are straightforward and follow in a similar way to Proposition 6.1 in
[
47
]
.
∎

6.2
Diagonalization of the linearized operator in the normal directions

This section is devoted to the reduction of the linearized operator
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\omega}
, defined in (
6.7
), to constant coefficients. This procedure is done in three steps. First, we reduce the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon r}
introduced in (
6.8
) up to smoothing reminders. Then we study the action of the localization in the normal directions. Finally, we almost eliminate the remainders by using a KAM reduction procedure.
We fix the following parameters.

\begin{array}[]{ll}s_{l}:=s_{0}+\tau_{1}q+\tau_{1}+2,&\overline{\mu}_{2}:=4\tau_{1}q+6\tau_{1}+3,\\
\overline{s}_{l}:=s_{l}+\tau_{2}q+\tau_{2},&\overline{s}_{h}:=\frac{3}{2}\overline{\mu}_{2}+s_{l}+1.\end{array}

(6.16)

6.2.1
Leading orders reduction

In this section, we shall straighten the transport part by using a suitable quasi-periodic symplectic change of variables and look at its conjugation action on the non-local terms. The reduction of the transport part is done by a KAM iterative scheme.
Such technique is now well-developed in
[
3
,
7
,
14
,
28
,
37
,
47
]
. The result reads as follows.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
(\gamma,q,d,\tau_{1},s_{0},\overline{\mu}_{2},s_{l},\overline{s}_{h},S)
satisfy (
1.11
), (
1.10
) and (
6.16
). Let
\upsilon\in\left(0,\frac{1}{q+2}\right].
We set

\sigma_{1}=s_{0}+\tau_{1}q+2\tau_{1}+4\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\sigma_{2}=s_{0}+\sigma_{1}+3.

(6.17)

For any
(\mu_{2},\mathtt{p},s_{h})
satisfying

\mu_{2}\geqslant\overline{\mu}_{2},\hskip 10.00002pt\mathtt{p}\geqslant 0,\hskip 10.00002pts_{h}\geqslant\max\left(\frac{3}{2}\mu_{2}+s_{l}+1,\overline{s}_{h}+\mathtt{p}\right),

(6.18)

there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that if

\varepsilon\gamma^{-1}N_{0}^{\mu_{2}}\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\|\mathfrak{I}_{0}\|_{q,s_{h}+\sigma_{2}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\leqslant 1,

(6.19)

then following assertions hold true.

1.

There exist

V_{i_{0}}^{\infty}\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},\mathbb{C})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\beta\in W^{q,\infty,\gamma}(\mathcal{O},H^{S})

such that with
\mathscr{B}
defined in (
4.6
) one gets the following results.

(i)

The function
V_{i_{0}}^{\infty}
satisfies the estimate:

\|V_{i_{0}}^{\infty}-\tfrac{1}{2}\|_{q}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\varepsilon.

(6.20)

(ii)

The transformations
\mathscr{B}^{\pm 1},\mathcal{B}^{\pm 1},{\beta}
and
\widehat{\beta}
satisfy the following estimates: for all
s\in[s_{0},S]
,

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{B}^{\pm 1}\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\mathcal{B}^{\pm 1}\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\rho\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\varepsilon\gamma^{-1}\|\mathfrak{I}_{0}\|_{q,s+\sigma_{1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\|\rho\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}},

(6.21)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\beta}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\|\beta\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\gamma^{-1}\left(1+\|\mathfrak{I}_{0}\|_{q,s+\sigma_{1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\right).

(6.22)

Moreover,
\beta
and
\widehat{\beta}
satisfy the following symmetry condition:

\beta(\mu,-\varphi,-\theta)=-\beta(\mu,\varphi,\theta)\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{\beta}(\mu,-\varphi,-\theta)=-\widehat{\beta}(\mu,\varphi,\theta).

(6.23)

(iii)

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then in the truncated Cantor set

\mathcal{O}_{\infty,n}^{\gamma,\tau_{1}}(i_{0})=\bigcap_{(l,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{(0,0)\}\atop|l|\leqslant N_{n}}\left\{(b,\omega)\in\mathcal{O}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{s.t.}\hskip 10.00002pt\big|\omega\cdot l+jV_{i_{0}}^{\infty}(b,\omega)\big|>\tfrac{4\gamma^{\upsilon}\langle j\rangle}{\langle l\rangle^{\tau_{1}}}\right\},

(6.24)

we have the decomposition

\mathfrak{L}_{\varepsilon r}:=\mathscr{B}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon r}\mathscr{B}=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}+V_{i_{0}}^{\infty}\partial_{\theta}+\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{K}_{b}\ast\cdot+\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{R}_{\varepsilon r}+\mathtt{E}_{n}^{0},

where
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon r}
is given by (
6.8
),
\mathcal{K}_{b}
is defined in Lemma
3.2
and
\mathtt{E}_{n}^{0}=\mathtt{E}_{n}^{0}(b,\omega,i_{0})
is a linear operator satisfying

\|\mathtt{E}_{n}^{0}\rho\|_{q,s_{0}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\varepsilon N_{0}^{\mu_{2}}N_{n+1}^{-\mu_{2}}\|\rho\|_{q,s_{0}+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(6.25)

The operator
\mathfrak{R}_{\varepsilon r}
is a real and reversibility preserving integral operator satisfying

\displaystyle\forall s\in[s_{0},S],\hskip 10.00002pt\max_{k\in\{0,1,2\}}\|\partial_{\theta}^{k}\mathfrak{R}_{\varepsilon r}\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\gamma^{-1}\left(1+\|\mathfrak{I}_{0}\|_{q,s+\sigma_{2}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\right).

(6.26)

2.

Given two tori
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
both satisfying (
6.19
), we have

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{12}V_{i}^{\infty}\|_{q}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\|\Delta_{12}i\|_{q,\overline{s}_{h}+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}},

(6.27)

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{12}\beta\|_{q,\overline{s}_{h}+\mathtt{p}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}+\|\Delta_{12}\widehat{\beta}\|_{q,\overline{s}_{h}+\mathtt{p}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\gamma^{-1}\|\Delta_{12}i\|_{q,\overline{s}_{h}+\mathtt{p}+\sigma_{1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(6.28)

In addition, we have

\displaystyle\max_{k\in\{0,1\}}\|\Delta_{12}(\partial_{\theta}^{k}\mathfrak{R}_{\varepsilon r})\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,\overline{s}_{h}+\mathtt{p}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\gamma^{-1}\|\Delta_{12}i\|_{q,\overline{s}_{h}+\mathtt{p}+\sigma_{2}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(6.29)

Proof.

Notice that along the proof, to simplify the notation, we shall omit the dependence with respect to the parameters
b,\omega
kipping in mind that the functions appearing actually depend on them.
We begin by setting

V_{0}=\tfrac{1}{2}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 10.00002ptf_{0}(\varphi,\theta):=V_{\varepsilon r}(\varphi,\theta)-\tfrac{1}{2},

(6.30)

with
V_{\varepsilon r}
defined by (
3.1
). According to (
6.10
) and (
3.4
), one gets

f_{0}(-\varphi,-\theta)=f_{0}(\varphi,\theta).

(6.31)

Notice that according to (
3.1
), (
5.48
) and Taylor formula, one has

\|f_{0}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\varepsilon\left(1+\|\mathfrak{I}_{0}\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\right).

(6.32)

These properties allow to apply
[
47
, Prop. 6.2]
, whose proof is based on a KAM iterative scheme reduction of the perturbation term
f_{0}
and construct
\beta
and
V_{i_{0}}^{\infty}.
In particular, for any
n\in\mathbb{N},
we are able to construct a Cantor set
\mathcal{O}_{\infty,n}^{\gamma,\tau_{1}}(i_{0})
in the form (
6.24
) in which the following reduction holds

\mathscr{B}^{-1}\big(\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}+\partial_{\theta}\big(V_{\varepsilon r}\cdot\big)\big)\mathscr{B}=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}+V_{i_{0}}^{\infty}\partial_{\theta}+\mathtt{E}_{n}^{0},

(6.33)

where
\mathtt{E}_{n}^{0}
is an operator enjoying the decay property stated in (
6.25
). Using (
6.33
), (
5.21
), (
5.22
) and Lemma
4.3
-(i), one obtains in the Cantor set
\mathcal{O}_{\infty,n}^{\gamma,\tau_{1}}(i_{0})
the following decomposition

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon r}\mathscr{B}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{B}^{-1}\big(\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}+\partial_{\theta}\left(V_{\varepsilon r}\cdot\right)\big)\mathscr{B}+\mathscr{B}^{-1}\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{L}_{\varepsilon r}\mathscr{B}-\mathscr{B}^{-1}\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{S}_{\varepsilon r}\mathscr{B}

\displaystyle=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}+V_{i_{0}}^{\infty}\partial_{\theta}+\mathscr{B}^{-1}\partial_{\theta}\big(\mathcal{K}_{1,b}\ast\cdot\big)\mathscr{B}+\mathscr{B}^{-1}\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{L}_{\varepsilon r,1}\mathscr{B}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt-\mathscr{B}^{-1}\partial_{\theta}\big(\mathcal{K}_{2,b}\ast\cdot\big)\mathscr{B}-\mathscr{B}^{-1}\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{S}_{\varepsilon r,1}\mathscr{B}+\mathtt{E}_{n}^{0}

\displaystyle=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}+V_{i_{0}}^{\infty}\partial_{\theta}+\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{K}_{1,b}\ast\cdot-\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{K}_{2,b}\ast\cdot+\partial_{\theta}\mathfrak{R}_{\varepsilon r}+\mathtt{E}_{n}^{0},

wehre

\mathfrak{R}_{\varepsilon r}:=\Big[\mathcal{B}^{-1}\big(\mathcal{K}_{1,b}\ast\cdot\big)\mathscr{B}-\mathcal{K}_{1,b}\ast\cdot\Big]-\Big[\mathcal{B}^{-1}\big(\mathcal{K}_{2,b}\ast\cdot\big)\mathscr{B}-\mathcal{K}_{2,b}\ast\cdot\Big]+\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathbf{L}_{\varepsilon r,1}\mathscr{B}-\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathbf{S}_{\varepsilon r,1}\mathscr{B}.

(6.34)

Direct computations using (
3.11
) lead to

\mathcal{B}^{-1}(\mathcal{K}_{1,b}\ast(\mathscr{B}\rho))(\varphi,\theta)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho(\varphi,\eta)\log(\mathscr{A}_{\widehat{\beta}}(\varphi,\theta,\eta))d\eta,

where

\mathscr{A}_{\widehat{\beta}}(\varphi,\theta,\eta):=\left|\sin\left(\tfrac{\eta-\theta}{2}+\widehat{h}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)\right)\right|\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with}\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{h}(\varphi,\theta,\eta):=\tfrac{\widehat{\beta}(\varphi,\theta)-\widehat{\beta}(\varphi,\eta)}{2}\cdot

Using elementary trigonometric identities, we can write

\mathscr{A}_{\widehat{\beta}}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)=\left|\sin\left(\tfrac{\eta-\theta}{2}\right)\right|v_{\widehat{\beta}}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{with}\hskip 10.00002ptv_{\widehat{\beta}}(\varphi,\theta,\eta):=\cos\big(\widehat{h}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)\big)+\tfrac{\sin\left(\widehat{h}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)\right)}{\tan\big(\tfrac{\eta-\theta}{2}\big)}\cdot

In view of (
6.23
), one finds that
v_{\widehat{\beta}}
enjoys the following symmetry property,

v_{\widehat{\beta}}(-\varphi,-\theta,-\eta)=v_{\widehat{\beta}}(\varphi,\theta,\eta).

(6.35)

Using the morphism property of the logarithm, one gets

\Big[\mathcal{B}^{-1}\big(\mathcal{K}_{1,b}\ast\rho\big)\mathscr{B}-\mathcal{K}_{1,b}\ast\rho\big](\varphi,\theta)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho(\varphi,\eta)\mathbb{K}_{\widehat{\beta},2}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)d\eta

where

\mathbb{K}_{\widehat{\beta},2}(\varphi,\theta,\eta):=\log\big(v_{\widehat{\beta}}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)\big).

(6.36)

Notice that (
6.36
) and (
6.35
) imply

\mathbb{K}_{\widehat{\beta},2}(-\varphi,-\theta,-\eta)=\mathbb{K}_{\widehat{\beta},2}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}.

(6.37)

Hence, we deduce from Lemma
4.7
that
\mathcal{B}^{-1}\big(\mathcal{K}_{1,b}\ast\cdot\big)\mathscr{B}-\mathcal{K}_{1,b}\ast\cdot
is a real and reversibility preserving Toeplitz in time operator.
Writting

v_{\widehat{\beta}}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)=1+\Big(\cos\big(\widehat{h}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)\big)-1\Big)+\tfrac{\sin\left(\widehat{h}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)\right)}{\tan\big(\tfrac{\eta-\theta}{2}\big)},

one finds, by Lemma
4.1
-(v), Lemma
4.2
and (
6.22
),

\displaystyle\|v_{\widehat{\beta}}-1\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\widehat{\beta}\|_{q,s+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\gamma^{-1}\left(1+\|\mathfrak{I}_{0}\|_{q,s+1+\sigma_{1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\right).

Moreover, by (
6.28
) and the Mean Value Theorem (applied with
\mathtt{p}
replaced by
\mathtt{p}+s_{0}+2
), we find

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{12}v_{\widehat{\beta}}\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{\overline{s}_{h}+\mathtt{p}+s_{0}+1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Delta_{12}\widehat{\beta}\|_{q,\overline{s}_{h}+\mathtt{p}+s_{0}+2}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\gamma^{-1}\|\Delta_{12}i\|_{q,\overline{s}_{h}+\mathtt{p}+s_{0}+2+\sigma_{1}}.

In a similar way, we deduce that

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{K}_{\widehat{\beta},2}\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\varepsilon\gamma^{-1}\left(1+\|\mathfrak{I}_{0}\|_{q,s+1+\sigma_{1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\right),

(6.38)

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{12}\mathbb{K}_{\widehat{\beta},2}\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{\overline{s}_{h}+\mathtt{p}+s_{0}+1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\varepsilon\gamma^{-1}\|\Delta_{12}i\|_{q,\overline{s}_{h}+\mathtt{p}+s_{0}+2+\sigma_{1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(6.39)

In view of Lemma
4.7
we get, from (
6.38
) and (
6.39
),

\displaystyle\max_{k\in\{0,1,2\}}\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{k}\Big[\mathcal{B}^{-1}\big(\mathcal{K}_{1,b}\ast\cdot\big)\mathscr{B}-\mathcal{K}_{1,b}\ast\cdot\Big]\Big\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\varepsilon\gamma^{-1}\left(1+\|\mathfrak{I}_{0}\|_{q,s+s_{0}+3+\sigma_{1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\right),

(6.40)

\displaystyle\max_{k\in\{0,1\}}\Big\|\Delta_{12}\partial_{\theta}^{k}\Big[\mathcal{B}^{-1}\big(\mathcal{K}_{1,b}\ast\cdot\big)\mathscr{B}-\mathcal{K}_{1,b}\ast\cdot\Big]\Big\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,\overline{s}_{h}+\mathtt{p}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\varepsilon\gamma^{-1}\|\Delta_{12}i\|_{q,\overline{s}_{h}+\mathtt{p}+s_{0}+2+\sigma_{1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(6.41)

According to (
3.12
), one finds

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{-1}\big(\mathcal{K}_{2,b}\ast(\mathscr{B}\rho)\big)(\varphi,\theta)-\mathcal{K}_{2,b}\ast\rho(\varphi,\theta)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho(\varphi,\eta)\mathscr{K}_{\widehat{\beta},2}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)d\eta,

with

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}_{\widehat{\beta},2}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)

\displaystyle:=\tfrac{1}{2}\Big[\log\big(1+b^{4}-2b^{2}\cos(\eta-\theta+\widehat{h}(\varphi,\theta,\eta))\big)-\log\big(1+b^{4}-2b^{2}\cos(\eta-\theta)\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\log\left(\tfrac{1+b^{4}-2b^{2}\cos\big(\eta-\theta+\widehat{h}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)\big)}{1+b^{4}-2b^{2}\cos(\eta-\theta)}\right).

From (
6.23
), we deduce that

\mathscr{K}_{\widehat{\beta},2}(-\varphi,-\theta,-\eta)=\mathscr{K}_{\widehat{\beta},2}(\varphi,\theta,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}.

(6.42)

It follows from Lemma
4.7
that
\mathcal{B}^{-1}\big(\mathcal{K}_{2,b}\ast\cdot\big)\mathscr{B}-\mathcal{K}_{2,b}\ast\cdot
is a real and reversibility preserving Toeplitz in time operator.
Arguying as for (
5.26
) and using (
6.22
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{K}_{\widehat{\beta},2}\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{s}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\widehat{\beta}\|_{q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\gamma^{-1}\left(1+\|\mathfrak{I}_{0}\|_{q,s+\sigma_{1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\right).

(6.43)

Using Mean Value theorem, applied with
\mathtt{p}
replaced by
\mathtt{p}+s_{0}+1
, one also gets by (
6.28
)

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{12}\mathscr{K}_{\widehat{\beta},2}\|_{q,H_{\varphi,\theta,\eta}^{\overline{s}_{h}+\mathtt{p}+s_{0}+1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Delta_{12}\widehat{\beta}\|_{q,\overline{s}_{h}+\mathtt{p}+s_{0}+1}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\gamma^{-1}\|\Delta_{12}i\|_{q,\overline{s}_{h}+\mathtt{p}+s_{0}+1+\sigma_{1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(6.44)

Consequently, in view of Lemma
4.7
, we get from (
6.2.1
)

\max_{k\in\{0,1,2\}}\Big\|\partial_{\theta}^{k}\Big[\mathcal{B}^{-1}\big(\mathcal{K}_{2,b}\ast\cdot\big)\mathscr{B}-\mathcal{K}_{2,b}\ast\cdot\Big]\Big\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,s}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\varepsilon\gamma^{-1}\left(1+\|\mathfrak{I}_{0}\|_{q,s+s_{0}+2+\sigma_{1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\right)

(6.45)

and from (
6.2.1
)

\max_{k\in\{0,1\}}\Big\|\Delta_{12}\partial_{\theta}^{k}\Big[\mathcal{B}^{-1}\big(\mathcal{K}_{2,b}\ast\cdot\big)\mathscr{B}-\mathcal{K}_{2,b}\ast\cdot\Big]\Big\|_{\textnormal{\tiny{O-d}},q,\overline{s}_{h}+\mathtt{p}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\varepsilon\gamma^{-1}\|\Delta_{12}i\|_{q,\overline{s}_{h}+\mathtt{p}+s_{0}+1+\sigma_{1}}^{\gamma,\mathcal{O}}.

(6.46)

Next, putting together (
5.23
), (
6.23
) and Lemma
4.8
, we infer that
\mathcal{B}^{-1}\mathbf{L}_{\varepsilon r,1}\mathscr{B}
